Step-by-step build

The build runs in three stages, with bench work in parallel (Figure 3). Phases 0 to 2 come first: prove your tools on the unmodified Touring, record its baseline, and test the donor while it still runs.

  • Stage 1, strip and metalwork (Phases 4–6). All welding happens while the car is stripped and the fuel tank is out: subframe mounts, floor and rear arches (and the doors, if you reshape them). You fit-check the M3 rear axle and muffler before you close the floor, and mock up the axle with your final springs, dampers, wheels and tires before you cut the arches.
  • Stage 2, mechanical (Phases 7–12). Axles, fuel, engine, cooling, exhaust, wiring and coding, then the first start and shakedown with the M3 front end fitted in primer.
  • Stage 3, finish (Phase 13). Final bodywork, paint, trim and inspection.

If you would rather paint once with the car empty, you can move paint to the end of Stage 1. You give up the chance to fix gaps and clearances found during the shakedown without touching fresh paint.

Time puts the phases on a calendar, with the bench work running alongside.

How to use the phases. Each phase says what to do and in what order, and links to the deep dives and the Wiring and coding manual for how. Torques are in Torque values and fluids in Fluids and capacities. Each phase ends with a Done when table: the checks, what a pass looks like, and what to do if one fails. Don't cover a phase's work with the next one until every check passes.

Keep the fallbacks. Hold on to the parts on the hold list in Space, storage and logistics, among them your Touring's DSC unit, brake booster, master cylinder and cluster, until the car has passed inspection. In one S54 Touring the M3's DSC unit never cleared its fault, and refitting the Touring's own unit cured it (e46fanatics). Plan where two cars' worth of parts will live first: Space, storage and logistics.

Watch the whole order: two DIY series film a complete conversion: Tracks and Trails – building an E46 M3 Touring (2025), 06:00, on a North American 325i Touring, and M346 Projects – SMG Touring build Pt 1 (2023), 06:40, on a US Touring with a convertible donor. Treat their exhaust as a warning, not a model: both cars ended up with catless headers (Tracks and Trails – first drive (2026), 47:00, M346 Projects – Euro header install (2024), 10:20). Keep the M3's catalysts and every other emissions part (Registration and legal).

Phase 0: Paperwork and baseline

  1. Decode your VIN and note the build date and option codes. Then confirm what you have (Check your own Touring first explains each check):
    • A rear-drive 325i, not an xi. If it's an xi, read If your car is a 325xi first.
    • An M54B25 engine with a plastic tank, not the M56 SULEV. BMW's bulletin introduced the M56 for 2003 with automatics, for California, New York and Massachusetts (Vermont from 2004). BMW's catalog can be read as listing a five-speed for the US 325i M56 Touring, but only in groups shared with other engines, and its M56 gearbox-module harness is for automatics only, so a manual M56 is probably a conversion. Either way a manual doesn't prove an M54: check the engine code and the tank. An M56 car has a pump controller, a metal tank and no return line, and the fuel plan doesn't apply (BMW B11 75 02, ETK: gearbox, ETK: pump controller).
    • The DME: an MS43 if built to 02/2003, an MS45.1 from 03/2003; confirm it on the label (ETK, manual). The re-pin list depends on it.
    • DSC, not ASC only. BMW lists ASC-only units for US Tourings, mostly up to 09/2001, and those cars have no steering-angle sensor (ETK).
    • Facelift or not. Tourings built from 09/2001 have the facelift front, which matters for fenders and bumper supports, and store their options as a vehicle order (FA) instead of the older key code (ZCS) (BMW ST406). That matters when an M3 module has to accept your car.
    • Climate control: automatic (IHKA) or manual (IHKR). A/C has only been confirmed working with the S54 on IHKA cars, so test it early on an IHKR car (e46fanatics).
    • The console switch panel. Some Tourings have only a DSC button; the M3 Sport button then needs the M3 switch unit and its power feeds as well as two DME wires (Tenza Motorsports – 5 things part 2 (2021), 08:45).
  2. Get the factory documentation (Factory documentation and coding kit). Pull wiring diagrams (WDS) with your Touring's VIN loaded for the body side and the donor's for the engine side. One Touring swapper found the diagrams changed once his car's VIN was loaded (e46fanatics).
  3. Ask your state what an engine change needs, and get the answer in writing: put the questions for your state program to it (Registration and legal). In Massachusetts, write to MassDEP and the RMV Title Division as set out in Massachusetts, and photograph your Touring's underhood emissions (VECI) label: its LEV, ULEV or SULEV category matters there. In California every engine change goes to a BAR Referee, and Smog Check fails modified DME software that doesn't match an OEM or CARB-approved configuration (BAR, BAR, 2021). Wherever software is checked, ask whether a VIN-matched EWS-delete flash passes before you choose your immobilizer route.
  4. Build and prove the coding kit on the unmodified Touring, before you change anything (Wiring and coding manual: before you start). The usual failures are a cable without pins 7 and 8 bridged (an E46's body modules talk on pin 8) and a COM port that doesn't match obd.ini (e46fanatics, NAM3). PA-Soft needs its own interface, and its driver is the step most likely to fail on Windows 10 and 11 (e46fanatics). The software circulates through forums and vendors, not BMW.
  5. Back up everything with the Touring's original modules fitted, as the manual's Session 1 and backup list describe: the FA or ZCS, with screenshots of the option list, production date and type code (e46fanatics); each module's coding file (FSW_PSW.TRC) for the cluster, light module (LCM), GM5, DSC, EWS and climate control (ECUWorx); the cluster and LCM EEPROMs, read with PA-Soft (e46fanatics); and every module's part number and fault list. Photograph the odometer, and note the mileage the cluster and the LCM each hold. With the DME still in, run its leak-test jobs and tag each X60004 wire with what it does: on an MS45.1 that settles which pin carries the leak-test pump (manual).
  6. Inspect your own shell. Check the rear axle carrier panel (RACP) at all four subframe mounts, top and bottom, against CMP's list of failure points (CMP, part 1, part 2). Check the rear arches (rust starts at the inner lip, Redish), sills, jacking points, battery box and the floor round the tank brackets. A badly cracked RACP changes the plan: BMW's 21 kg replacement panel (41117000246, ETK) is reported discontinued (Turner, unverified).
  7. Record the baseline: on level ground, at normal ride height and placard tire pressures, measure at each corner the heights of the hub center and the arch lip, as you'll measure the donor in Phase 1, and note the fuel level. It's your reference for the arches and for any state rule on ride height (Massachusetts). Then the last alignment printout, and photos of the engine bay, the DME box (E-box), the harness routing and the underside. Check that the A/C blows cold, and book a shop to recover its refrigerant before teardown (Phase 4).

Watch: 50sKid – coding the vehicle order (2017), 08:52 reads the FA from an E46's cluster.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Coding kit INPA shows two solid black dots and reads the DME, cluster, LCM, EWS and DSC; NCS Expert reads the FA or ZCS Bridge pins 7 and 8, match the COM port, try another INPA build. Don't back up or code anything until this works
Backups FA or ZCS screenshots, a coding file per module, cluster and LCM EEPROM files and fault lists, stored in two places Repeat. Never change a module you haven't backed up
Your car Engine code, tank, DME, DSC, climate control and switch panel recorded M56, xi or ASC only: re-plan the fuel, axle or DSC parts before you buy a donor
Shell RACP, arches, sills and jacking points inspected and photographed Cracks: plan their repair for Phase 5. Torn or rotten floor: get a body-shop quote before you buy the donor
Your state Written answer on what the engine change needs No answer yet: don't buy the donor

Phase 1: Buy and inspect the donor

  1. Shortlist manual M3s that meet the model-year rule (Choose the donor). Ask for the VIN, build date and a photo of the engine number. If you might use its DSC unit, prefer one built 09/2002 or later. From then the US M3 has an MK60 with the same 47-pin plug as a Touring built from 09/2001; earlier M3s have an MK20 with a 43-pin plug and a separate pre-charge pump (ETK, from 09/2002, ETK, to 09/2002).
  2. Inspect with the checklist in Choose the donor. Do a compression test and a leak-down and compare the cylinders. One compilation gives about 11–12 bar minimum for the S54, about the same on every cylinder (Flow torque compilation, p.47; confirm in TIS).
  3. Confirm the panels you need are straight and sound: hood, fenders, headlights, bumper, both rear arch areas including the inner lips, and the trunk floor. M3 inner arches rust from the inside (Redish); one builder's donor lost its inner arch as soon as it was stripped (Autoalex Cars – Part 1 (2023), 07:40).
  4. Collect the title or salvage paperwork and a bill of sale. Photograph the engine number and VIN plates; inspectors and insurers may ask for them.
  5. Before teardown, while it runs, record what you can't test later. How to run each test, and what to look for, is in the Test it while it still runs table:
    • Modules: every module's part number, software version and fault list, read with INPA. Note whether the DSC is an MK60 or an MK20.
    • DME: its identity, fault memory and AIF data (the record that holds the VIN), read with Tool32 and saved before it goes to a vendor (ECUWorx).
    • Cluster: its EEPROM, read with PA-Soft and kept untouched (e46fanatics).
    • VANOS: Tool32's timing and leak tests on a warm engine, to compare with after the refresh (MSS54 job reference).
    • Fuel pressure: 5.0 ± 0.2 bar (72.5 ± 2.9 psi) (Bentley, quoted on e46fanatics).
    • Road test: every gear without a grind, no diff clunk, clutch, steering, brakes, ABS and DSC, and A/C that blows cold.
    • Labels: photos of the B-pillar tire placard and the underhood A/C and emissions labels.
    • Arch targets: on a level floor, at all four corners, the heights of the hub center, the arch lip and a body point both cars share, such as the floor beside each rear subframe mount, and how far the lip sits outboard of the tire's sidewall. Subtract to get each corner's hub-to-body and hub-to-lip figures: they give you the M3's stance and where your new arches go in Phase 6 (Measure and mock up before you cut).
  6. Book refrigerant recovery for the donor while it can still be driven or towed to a shop.

Watch: Autoalex Cars – Part 1 (2023), 00:40 inspects a donor M3 on a ramp: split brake hoses, a bulging tire, and a trunk floor cracked by an old impact on the rear brace.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Engine Compression and leak-down even across cylinders; VANOS tests and fuel pressure recorded Low or uneven: budget a head or engine job, or walk away
Electronics record Module IDs and faults, DME identity and AIF, and cluster EEPROM saved Repeat before anything is unplugged
DSC Unit type noted: MK60 or MK20 MK20: plan to keep your Touring's DSC (option A)
Measurements Hub, lip and body-point heights and lip offsets at four corners, with hub-to-body and hub-to-lip worked out; placard and labels photographed Measure before the car is lifted or stripped
Paperwork Title or salvage paperwork and bill of sale in hand; engine number and VIN plates photographed Don't buy without them

Phase 2: Strip the donor

  1. Have a shop recover the A/C refrigerant before you open any line; venting it is prohibited (EPA). Drain the engine oil, coolant, gearbox, diff, power-steering and brake fluid, and the fuel tank, into approved containers, and dispose of them properly (Safety).

  2. Label both halves of every connector with matching tags and photograph everything before it comes apart. Bag and tag fasteners by assembly. Support or strap the car before the engine or an axle comes out: its balance shifts on stands or a lift (Safety).

  3. Work down the Parts list and tag every Donor M3 line as it comes off. Easy to miss:

    • Cabin: cluster; the console switch unit with the Sport button (61316925520 on a coupe, 61316925521 on a convertible; ETK, ETK); the M3 clutch-switch module (61319122700, ETK); accelerator pedal; shift lever and carrier; mirrors; and the steering wheel if you want it (disarm the airbag per BMW's procedure first).
    • Engine bay: the complete engine harness with the DME, its gearbox-module harness and the whole E-box branch: the fuse carrier, the E-box plugs X6011, X6021, X6053 and X6054, and the battery feed X64101/X64102 (manual). Also the brackets for the expansion tank and secondary-air pump, which a non-M body doesn't have (Tracks and Trails – first drive (2026), 23:20); radiator, both fans, oil cooler and lines; power-steering reservoir, lines and cooler; A/C lines; and the DSC unit with its lines and the master cylinder, plus the brake booster if you plan DSC option B.
    • Underside: the fuel filter-regulator with its vacuum hoses, and the M3 fuel lines (16122229409, 16122229410, 16122229411, ETK); the complete exhaust with hangers and heat shields; the front heat shields, which differ on the M3 (Project BMW – S54 into a Touring (2021), 00:20); the driveshaft; the clutch hard line; and the M3 front brake hoses (34322229723), which are M3-only (ETK), though new ones are better (Brakes and DSC). The brake pipes can stay with the shell: an M3 built from 09/2002 uses the same pipes as your Touring (Brakes and bleeding).
    • Engine and gearbox: with a lift you can drop the front subframe with the engine still on it, as one UK builder did (PistonHeads); otherwise remove the gearbox and lift the engine out the top. Keep the gear-identification switch (23142229113) on the 420G; cruise control needs it (NAM3).
    • Front axle and steering: the rack with its tie rods and lines, control arms and brackets, struts, knuckles, hubs, brakes, sway bar and links, and the front reinforcement plate.
    • Rear axle as one unit (FCP Euro, part 1): subframe, diff, trailing arms with brakes and parking-brake shoes, half-shafts, sway bar, the tubular push rod (33312229295) and the V-brace (33312229899) (ETK). If you'll fit the V-brace, also cut out its body mounting boxes with their reinforcement panels (Phase 5).
    • Wheel-speed sensors, from an MK60 donor (built from 09/2002): the same active type as your Touring's, so keep them as spares. An MK20 donor's are inductive sensors that don't fit your harness or suit your MK60 (manual). That's why a 2004 Touring with a 2002 donor found the plugs didn't mate (Tenza Motorsports – 5 things part 2 (2021), 03:15), while a 2003 xi Touring took the M3 sensors (e46fanatics, Driveline and rear axle).

  4. Remove the front panels: hood, fenders, headlights, bumper with its carrier and supports, kidney grilles and liners.

  5. Cut out the metal you'll graft (Rear arches and doors), drilling out spot welds where you can instead of cutting:

    • both rear arch areas with generous margins: the outer skin plus as much of the inner wheel tub as you can get, or "you're going to have a gap" (M346 Projects – Touring build Pt 14, body work (2024), 33:00);
    • the fuel filler flap with its surround;
    • the trunk floor around the muffler, with its front reinforcement (51717894229) and the muffler brackets (41122494844, 41112493931) (ETK).

    Before you cut the arches, make cardboard templates of each flare at three or four stations. They show up accident damage now and help you check shape and symmetry in Phase 6.

  6. Sell what you won't use, such as SMG parts, the donor interior and glass, and a convertible's top. Keep the donor's DSC unit until your DSC plan has worked.

  7. Dispose of the shell and its title the way your state requires (Space, storage and logistics).

Watch: DBO – pulling an E46 M3 engine (2019), 27:01; M346 Projects – SMG Touring build Pt 1 (2023), 06:40 cuts the quarters and trunk floor from a convertible donor.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Parts Every Donor M3 line on the parts list removed, tagged, photographed and stored dry Missing or damaged: order it now, while you can still compare it with the donor
Fluids Refrigerant recovered (receipt kept); all fluids drained and disposed of Recover before any A/C line is opened
Body steel Arches with inner tubs, filler surround and floor section cut with margins; templates made Rotten or damaged donor steel: plan new BMW or aftermarket panels now (Rear arches and doors)
Shell Shell and title disposed of as your state requires Keep the donor's paperwork and engine-number photos either way

Phase 3: Bench work

This runs alongside Phases 4 to 8. The axles must be ready for Phases 7 and 8, the engine for Phase 9, the DME for Phase 11 and the fenders for Phase 12.

  1. Refresh the S54 on a stand (S54 refresh on the stand): rod bearings, VANOS, valve clearances, seals, rear main seal, water pump, thermostat and engine mounts.
    • Identify the rod bolts by their heads, not the build date. E-Torx (E12) heads are M10 bolts: always new, and tightened in three full cycles. Twelve-point (12 mm) heads are M11 bolts: reused, each in its own hole, and tightened once. BMW warns that not following the M10 procedure "will result in serious engine damage" (BMW SI B11 04 04).
    • Check the clearances with Plastigauge using the old bolts, and fit new M10 bolts only for final assembly: each check would use up one of a new bolt's tightening cycles. BMW's figure is 0.040–0.070 mm (BMW TIS 11 24 571). Some videos quote 0.03–0.07 mm; use BMW's.
  2. Clutch and flywheel: new flywheel bolts and pressure-plate screws (both single-use) and a new pilot bearing (Torque values, Consumables and single-use parts).
  3. Service the 420G while it's out: new input, selector-shaft and output seals (ETK printout in the Flow compilation, p.74), the gear-identification switch if it's worn, and new shifter bushings. Fill it in Phase 9, once it's in the car and level.
  4. Rebuild the axles: M3 subframe and diff mounts, rear trailing-arm bushings, worn arms, ball joints and end links, and the brakes. Fit the rear control arms to the subframe before the diff: one arm bolt can't go in with the diff in place (Tracks and Trails – perfecting the Touring's rear end (2025), 33:20). Keep powder coat out of the bushing bores. If a diff output flange comes out, mark its bolt first and refit it to the mark, which keeps the bearing preload (Flow compilation, pp.80–81). Fit the diff to the subframe here and torque its mounts on the bench, where you can reach them: front M14 bolt 200 Nm (148 ft-lb), rear M12 Torx bolts 20 Nm + 90° (Driveline and rear axle). Leave the rubber-bushed joints snug; you torque them at ride height in Phase 7.
  5. Send the DME away if you chose the delete route (Immobilizer (EWS), Shop briefs). Order the EWS delete and your Touring's full VIN. If the donor was an SMG car, add the manual (HS) software or a transmission-swap change: coding alone left one swapped car's SMG DME in limp mode (UNRIVALED – watch this before you S54 swap (2021), 02:02, NAM3). Decline every secondary-air, O2-sensor, catalyst and EVAP delete. Don't accept a Euro software flash either: it may drop US-only emissions functions such as the fuel-tank leak test, whose pins the DME tables mark "USA" (pinout sheet). The alternative, syncing the DME to your EWS, works only with a virgin DME: a used one has its rolling-code table burned in for good (BMW ST051).
  6. Prepare the M3 cluster now if you use a cluster service: your VIN, set to take your car's true mileage, and the rear-door warning LEDs that M3 clusters lack (e46fanatics, Instrument cluster and mileage). Otherwise do it in Phase 11.
  7. Graft the M3 front fenders off the car, if you're using genuine fenders (Front end on a 4-door body).
  8. Driveshaft: bolt a new flex disc to the shaft's front flange, with the M3's bolts and new self-locking nuts, at 115 Nm, turning the bolt and not the nut; its gearbox side waits for Phase 9. Fit a new center mount (Driveline and rear axle).
  9. Choose and buy the springs, dampers, wheels and tires you'll run (Suspension for Touring weight, Wheels, track and offsets); Phase 6 builds the arches around them. One S54 Touring on moved-over M3 coupe suspension sat so low that its rear tires rubbed (Tenza Motorsports – coilovers (2022), 00:40).

Watch: FCP Euro – S54 rod bearings (2022), 20:51 shows the three M10 cycles; Paul Vo – rod bearing torque procedure (2017), 08:00; ECS Tuning – S54 VANOS rebuild (2025), 17:30, which fits E39 M5 (S62) diaphragm springs and plates; FCP Euro – S54 valve adjustment (2022), 17:19; FCP Euro – E46 M3 clutch (2022), 17:19; FCP Euro – E46 M3 control arms and bushings (2020), 12:23.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Rod bearings Plastigauge reads 0.040–0.070 mm on every rod (old bolts); final bolts fitted and tightened to the procedure for their type Out of range: measure the crank journals. BMW lists 0.25 mm undersize shells (ETK), which means a machine shop
VANOS New seals and oil-pump disc; exhaust-hub tabs intact or the hub replaced (Beisan); the cam bridge sits flat and the crank pin slides in after reassembly (ECS Tuning – S54 VANOS rebuild (2025), 55:11) Re-time before the covers go on
Valves Intake 0.18–0.23 mm, exhaust 0.28–0.33 mm, measured below 35 °C (BMW S54 module) Re-shim
Clutch and 420G Flywheel and pressure plate on new bolts, torqued and marked; three new gearbox seals; gear switch fitted Fix it now; later it's a gearbox-out job
Axles Bushings, mounts and arms renewed; the diff on the subframe with its mount bolts torqued; bolts torqued and marked, rubber-bushed joints left snug Re-press any bushing that went in crooked
DME Back from the vendor with a written list of changes: EWS delete, your VIN, manual software if needed, and nothing else Any emissions function removed: send it back
Running gear Springs, dampers, wheels and tires in hand for the Phase 6 mock-up Don't start Phase 6 without them

Phase 4: Strip your Touring

  1. Have a shop recover the Touring's A/C refrigerant before you open any line (EPA), and cap every opening.
  2. Disconnect the battery. Drain the oil, coolant, power-steering fluid and brake fluid.
  3. Drain the fuel. Run the tank as low as you can beforehand, then pump the rest into approved containers, outdoors or with good ventilation and an extinguisher at hand (Safety). The E46 tank is a saddle tank whose left half empties through a siphon jet driven by the return flow (BMW ST055), so check that half separately.
  4. Remove the exhaust, driveshaft, M54 engine and gearbox, front suspension and steering, and rear axle. Two Touring builders took the front end off and pulled the engine out of the front (Tracks and Trails – M54 removal (2025), 01:00, Project BMW – S54 swap in a driveway (2021), 00:20). Support or strap the car before each heavy part comes out, and again after: the balance shifts (Safety).
  5. Drop the fuel tank and remove the filler neck, which runs right past the right-rear arch you'll be welding. Cap every line, and remove the EVAP parts near that arch; a fuel expansion-tank bracket sits among the rear wheelhouse parts (ETK). BimmerWorld calls tank removal "recommended, but not required" for the subframe welding alone (BimmerWorld); with the arches to weld as well, take it out.
  6. Remove the rear seats, cargo-floor trim and carpet, rear trim, rear door cards, wheel-arch liners and battery. Strip sound-deadening back from every weld line.
  7. Remove the M54 harness and DME for resale. The body harness stays.
  8. Sell the M54, 5-speed, MS43 or MS45.1 DME and engine harness, the 325i axle parts and the Touring's front panels. Keep the fallback parts in the hold list under Space, storage and logistics.
  9. Pressure-wash the underside and let it dry, then look again with everything off: the RACP from above and below, the rails, the inner arches, and the floor behind the tank and brake-pipe brackets, where builders have found trapped rust (Autoalex Cars – Part 3 (2023), 12:40).

Watch: Tracks and Trails – M54 removal (2025), 06:20 supports the rear once the engine is out.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
A/C Refrigerant recovered (receipt kept); every opening capped Already empty: note it. The drier is replaced in Phase 10 anyway
Fuel Both halves of the tank drained; tank, filler neck and EVAP parts out and stored away from the work area Fuel left in the left half: drain it through its sender opening
Support Shell stable on stands or the lift with the engine and axles out Re-position the supports before anyone works under it
Fallback parts Every part on the hold list in Space, storage and logistics labelled and stored Don't sell them yet
Inspection Underside clean and dry; new findings photographed and added to Phase 5 Plan the extra repairs before you buy plates

Phase 5: Metalwork 1, subframe mounts and floor

This is the point of no return: once you cut into your Touring's floor, it can't easily go back to stock, so finish Phases 0–4 and be sure of the plan first. The reinforcement and the floor are one job. The tops of the rear mounts sit at the front corners of the spare-wheel well, so opening the well gives you access to them (CMP, part 2). Rear subframe mount reinforcement has the procedure, and Exhaust and the spare-wheel well has the floor options and cut rules. Below is the order. The floor closes only after the axle and muffler have been tried in place.

If you're keeping the well and fitting a muffler in the Touring's own position, as some S54 Touring builders do (e46fanatics), reach the mount tops through trunk-floor windows in step 5 and close them with the kit's patch plates in step 9 (BimmerWorld). Skip step 8.

  1. Get the welding right first (Welding settings and safety). Weld test coupons in the real panel thicknesses and break them. Set up fume extraction and a fire watch, and keep every fuel and EVAP part away from the work.
  2. Measure the body diagonals across the rear and the front subframe mounting points, and write them down (Welding settings and safety shows how). BMW's body dimensions are in TIS group 51 00 (newTIS).
  3. Clean to bare metal and repair what Phases 0 and 4 found: stop-drill and weld cracks, and re-weld failed spot welds.
  4. Weld the underside plates, with the kit's spacers as its instructions say.
  5. Open the spare-wheel well by the cut rules, keeping the RACP and the flanges along both rails whole. Drilling out the factory spot welds keeps the flanges intact; for a donor-floor graft, drill out the same welds on both floors (M346 Projects – Touring build Pt 14, body work (2024), 00:40).
  6. Weld from above through the opening: the three rosette welds on top of each rear mount (BimmerWorld), any failed rail-to-floor spot welds, and topside rail plates if you fit them; CMP's list the Touring (CMP). Some kits' cabin-side pieces won't fit, because a Touring has no partition wall behind the rear seat (Calvin's Car Diary – PT15 (2025), 30:00). Then the front mounts: their top welds sit under the rear seat, and reaching them "requires cutting into the floor of the cabin" (CMP, part 2). Weld a patch over the opening afterwards.
  7. V-brace, if you'll fit it. A Touring has grommets where the M3 has threaded mounts for its V-brace, so builders weld in mounting boxes cut from the donor or bought (Autoalex Cars – Part 3 (2023), 05:00, Tracks and Trails – perfecting the Touring's rear end (2025), 06:00). Weld them in now, with the rest of the underside work.
  8. Fit-check before you close the floor. Clamp or cleco the new floor section in, bolt up the M3 rear subframe with its diff, and hang the M3 muffler. Check its clearance to the floor and the subframe, and where the hangers fall. The M3 muffler hangs from two mounts on the left and one on the right; the Touring floor has one on the left and none on the right, so weld the donor's brackets in now (Redish Motorsport – Touring build Part 1 (2022), 06:00). Then take the axle and muffler out again.
  9. Close the floor with the donor's M3 section, a new BMW M3 floor (still sold in 2023, Autoalex Cars – Part 3 (2023), 13:20) or a ribbed flat panel no thinner than the original, welded to every flange at about the factory spot-weld spacing (Exhaust and the spare-wheel well). Check that the battery tray and cargo-floor supports still fit.
  10. Measure the diagonals again and compare.
  11. Protect everything: weld-through or epoxy primer, seam sealer on both sides, underbody coating outside and cavity wax in the box sections. No structural foam while there's welding still to come; it makes welding "more difficult and dangerous" (BimmerWorld).

Watch: Tracks and Trails – RACP plates on a Touring (2025), 07:20; M346 Projects – Touring build Pt 14, body work (2024), 00:40 swaps in the M3 floor; Tracks and Trails – perfecting the Touring's rear end (2025), 03:20 shows the muffler hangers.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Test coupons Broken coupons tear the metal, not the weld; plug welds penetrate without blowing holes Change the settings and repeat before you weld the car
Repairs Every crack stop-drilled and welded; failed spot welds re-welded Grind out and re-weld
Reinforcement Underside plates, rear-mount rosette welds, front-mount top welds and any rail plates and V-brace boxes done, with penetration visible from inside where you can see Grind out and re-weld before any sealer goes on
Fit check M3 subframe and muffler clear the new floor; hanger brackets line up Move the bracket or reshape the floor before closing
Diagonals Match your before-welding figures Stop. Compare with BMW's body dimensions in TIS and get a body shop with a jig to check
Protection All seams sealed both sides, primed, coated and cavity-waxed Finish it now; it won't be reachable later

Phase 6: Metalwork 2, rear arches

  1. Mock up the rear axle as Measure and mock up before you cut describes, with your final dampers, wheels and tires (Suspension for Touring weight) and the springs out. Jack each hub until its hub-to-body height matches the donor's from Phase 1, or your own target if your springs will set the car higher or lower. No BMW ride-height figure was found for this car, so the donor is your reference.
  2. Find full bump: with the spring still out, jack each hub until the bump stop is fully compressed, and check the tire's position there. The new arch has to clear it.
  3. Mark the targets: where each new lip must sit around its tire, at the donor's hub-to-lip height and lip offset, checked from the car's center line so both sides match.
  4. Check the donor steel against your templates. BMW gives the M3 convertible the same rear track and body width as the coupe, so its arch almost certainly matches (BMW USA), but compare before you cut. One builder's donor arches had accident damage and curled in at the bottom (M346 Projects – Touring build Pt 14, body work (2024), 58:40).
  5. Weld in the arches (Rear arches and doors): the outer skin; the inner arch extended, or the donor's inner tub grafted; the gaps at the rocker and door filled; and the front of the flare dressed in, because the coupe's flare sits wider than a four-door body there (M346 Projects – Touring build Pt 14, body work (2024), 28:20). Graft the M3 filler flap and surround with the right-side arch.
  6. Before you seal the right quarter, trial-fit the Touring's filler neck to the M3 filler pot. The new arch sits about 25–30 mm further out (HARD Motorsport, e46fanatics), and a neck that doesn't reach or seal will fail the EVAP leak test.
  7. Decide the doors with them shut and aligned: leave them standard and stop the flare at the shut line, as BMW did, or reshape their trailing edges (Rear arches and doors).
  8. Epoxy-prime, seam-seal and cavity-wax. Leave filler work for Stage 3.
  9. Remove the mock-up axle.

Watch: M346 Projects – Touring build Pt 14, body work (2024), 27:20 grafts the inner tub; Tracks and Trails – bodywork (2026), 12:40; M346 Projects – Pt 22 door flare (2025), 00:20.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Symmetry Lip heights above the hub centers, and lip offsets from the center line, match side to side Re-check the mock-up corner heights before you rework metal
Bump clearance The tire clears the lip and inner arch with the bump stop fully compressed Rework the inner arch or lip now
Doors Doors open and close freely; shut gaps match the originals (feeler gauges) Rework the door edge or the flare's leading edge
Filler The neck reaches and seals in the M3 filler pot; the flap opens and closes Rework before the quarter is sealed
Inner lip A sealed, double-skin edge all round Weld and seal the gaps before primer

Phase 7: Fit the M3 rear axle and fuel system

Do the fuel system first, while the tank is still easy to drop again.

  1. Pump: keep your Touring's pump and test it, or fit the M3 pump with the M3 baffle. Owners have fitted both into sedan tanks, which are the same part as the Touring's, though the outlet fitting differs (e46fanatics, ETK). With your own pump, check that the M3 rear feed line's quick-disconnect fits its outlet, or keep your rear feed line and adapt at the filter (Fuel system). Refit the tank.
  2. Filter-regulator and lines: fit the M3 filter, regulator housing and 5-bar regulator on your Touring's bracket, and the M3 chassis lines or adapted fittings. The M3 uses quick-disconnect fittings where the Touring has barbs and clamps (e46fanatics). Check feed and return before you connect them: swapping them caused one swap's no-start (NAM3). Route the regulator's vacuum hose toward the engine's air distribution pipe (Fuel system).
  3. Test the pump at 5 bar before anything covers it. Bridge the K96 relay socket with a relay-bypass switch, as BMW's own test does, and put a gauge on the end of the front feed line in the engine bay (BMW ST041, part 2). Check the relay socket's pins on WDS for your VIN.
  4. Raise the rear axle on a table or jack, keeping the subframe level as it goes up over the studs (Tenza Motorsports – M3 rear end (2021), 04:40). The subframe hangs on four mounts: at each front mount an M12×1.5 stud screwed into the body (90 Nm) with a self-locking collar nut (77 Nm), and at each rear mount one M12×1.5×116 bolt (77 Nm). Torque the studs before the axle goes up, line up the front studs before the rear bolt holes, set the push rod with the kit's spacer plates over the front studs under their collar nuts, then torque the nuts and rear bolts and the push rod's bolts (Driveline and rear axle). Fit the V-brace if you fitted its mounts.
  5. Fit springs and dampers rated for the Touring, the M3 sway bar and links, the half-shafts with new bolts, brake lines, your own wheel-speed sensors (or an MK60 donor's) (Driveline and rear axle), and your parking-brake cables. Torque the joints that don't need ride height as they go in: the trailing-arm brackets to the body and the two ball-joint bolts at each trailing arm.
  6. Adjust the parking-brake shoes (Brakes and bleeding). You bed them in during the shakedown.
  7. Fill the diff with BMW's limited-slip oil (Fluids and capacities).
  8. Torque the rubber-bushed joints at loaded ride height, so the bushings aren't pre-twisted: the trailing-arm through-bolts, the control-arm bolts at the subframe and the damper lower bolts, which BMW's diagrams mark (Flow compilation, pp.16–17), and the anti-roll bar's brackets and links, which are rubber-mounted too (Driveline and rear axle). With the body on stands, jack under the axle until the car just starts to lift off them (Tracks and Trails – perfecting the Touring's rear end (2025), 40:20). Paint-mark every fastener.

Watch: Tracks and Trails – perfecting the Touring's rear end (2025), 35:00 raises the M3 axle into a Touring and torques the bushed joints at ride height.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Fuel pressure 5.0 ± 0.2 bar (72.5 ± 2.9 psi) static; above 4.5 bar (65 psi) 20 minutes after switch-off (Bentley, quoted on e46fanatics) Bleeds down more than 0.5 bar: clamp the hoses to find whether the pump's check valve or the regulator leaks, as BMW's test does (BMW ST041, part 2). Low pressure: regulator O-rings, filter, then the pump
Fuel flow At least 1.12 L in 30 s at 12 V, collected as TIS 13 31 describes (Bentley, quoted on e46fanatics) Replace the pump: a Touring pump, or the M3 pump and baffle
Fuel leaks None at any joint with the system pressurised; feed and return confirmed Fix before the axle goes in
Rear axle Studs, collar nuts and rear bolts torqued; every fastener paint-marked; the rubber-bushed joints done at loaded height; push rod on its spacers Re-torque at the right height
Diff Filled to the fill plug Top up, check the plugs' seals
Parking brake Rear wheels turn freely with the lever down and lock with it up Re-adjust the shoes

Phase 8: Fit the M3 front suspension and steering

  1. Fit the M3 rack, control arms and brackets, struts, knuckles, hubs and brakes to your front subframe (or the donor's). If you're installing the donor's subframe with the engine on it, combine this with Phase 9. If you replace the front hubs (31222229501), each takes a new collar nut at 290 Nm (214 ft-lb) (Torque values).
  2. Center the steering: wheel straight ahead, and the notch in the steering shaft lined up with the coupler's bolt hole (FCP Euro – S54 rod bearings (2022), 39:54). Fit a new pinch bolt (BMW SI B11 04 04). Before you fit a replacement rack, check that its hard lines clear the subframe; one aftermarket rack's didn't (50sKid – rack replacement (2018), 23:35).
  3. Connect the power-steering lines, fit the sway bar and end links, and fit the front reinforcement plate with new bolts.
  4. Brakes: fit the parts for the DSC option you chose in DSC, brakes and steering angle. Option A keeps your Touring's DSC unit, booster and master cylinder and adds the M3 front brake hoses (ETK). Your brake pipes stay: from 09/2002 the M3 uses the same front pipes as your Touring, and the rear pipes and hoses are the same on both cars (Brakes and bleeding). Option B fits the M3 DSC unit with its master cylinder. With an MK20 donor, use option A.
  5. Test, fill and bleed: test new hard lines with low-pressure air first, about 20 psi (NAM3), then fill and bleed conventionally (Brakes and bleeding). A dry or replaced DSC unit also needs INPA's pump routine once it can talk to INPA (Phase 11).
  6. Leave the front rubber-bushed joints snug. You torque them at ride height once the engine is in (Phase 12).

Watch: FCP Euro – E46 M3 control arms and bushings (2020), 15:58; 50sKid – rack replacement (2018), 12:35, on a non-M car with the same rack mounting.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Fasteners Torqued and paint-marked, except the rubber-bushed joints, which stay snug for Phase 12 Re-torque
Steering Wheel straight with the rack centered; full lock both ways without binding or line contact Re-index the coupler; re-route lines
Brake lines Air test holds; after filling, no weeps at any union with the pedal held hard Re-make the union; replace crushed flares
Pedal High and firm after bleeding Bleed again before you go on

Phase 9: Install the S54 and 420G

  1. Bolt the engine and gearbox together with the clutch and flywheel you fitted in Phase 3. Fit the M3 engine arms, new mounts and the 420G's gearbox support. Fit the secondary-air valve before the engine goes in (M3projekts – Ep5 (2023), 14:00).
  2. Lower the engine in from above, or raise it in on the subframe. Unbolt the oil-cooler lines so the cooler clears the crossmember (Project BMW – S54 on jack stands (2021), 01:20). One Touring builder had to drop the front subframe to get the S54 in (Tracks and Trails – first drive (2026), 55:40).
  3. Before you tighten the engine mounts, check the gap between the oil pan and the steering rack and subframe. Route the power-steering lines clear of the pan and sway bar: one M3 owner's lines wore a mark into his pan (Paul Vo – rod bearing torque procedure (2017), video description).
  4. Fit the shift carrier and lever with new bushings, then the complete M3 driveshaft: bolt its flex disc to the gearbox's output flange at 115 Nm (the shaft side was done in Phase 3), and fit the center mount (Driveline and rear axle).
  5. Fit the M3 clutch hard line and bleed the clutch (Brakes and bleeding). Clamping the flexible hose while you change lines keeps most of the air out (Throttle House – E46 clutch delay valve removal (2016), 00:26).
  6. Fill the 420G to its fill plug with the car level (Fluids and capacities).

Watch: Project BMW – S54 into a Touring (2021), 05:20 installs the engine and gearbox together; FCP Euro – E46 M3 clutch (2022), 26:34 fits the 420G.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Oil pan Clear of the rack and subframe; power-steering lines clear of the pan and sway bar Check the engine sits on the M3 arms correctly; re-route the lines
Mounts Engine and gearbox mounts torqued and marked Re-torque
Clutch Pedal firm and returns; the slave's pushrod moves its full stroke (check from below with a helper) Bleed again; bench-bleed the slave (Brakes and bleeding)
Driveshaft Flex disc and center mount torqued; center bearing preloaded forward (the Flow compilation gives 4–6 mm, p.79) Loosen, preload and re-torque
Gearbox Filled to the fill plug, no leaks at the seals Fix a leaking seal now

Phase 10: Cooling, intake and exhaust

  1. Fit the radiator, both fans, the oil cooler and lines, and a new expansion tank (Cooling and oil cooler). The M3 expansion tank sits on the other side of the engine bay from the Touring's, on the donor brackets from Phase 2.
  2. Fit the intake and airbox, and the power-steering reservoir, lines and cooler. The M3's brake-booster vacuum hose connects on the opposite side (Tenza Motorsports – 5 things (2021), 07:40).
  3. Route every sensor lead now. The coolant-level sensor moves sides with the tank, the M3 washer tank sits low behind the bumper, and the radiator-outlet sensor lead has to reach the M3 radiator's sensor. Your 325i already reads that sensor; the one build that had to extend the lead was a Euro 318d diesel Touring (Tenza Motorsports – 5 things (2021), 00:40, M3projekts – Ep6 (2023), 07:31). Extend leads with proper terminals or soldered, heat-shrunk joints, and list each one for the Phase 11 checks.
  4. A/C: fit the lines with new O-rings and a new drier, and keep the system capped for the shop. The drier and condenser are the same parts on both cars (ETK, M3, ETK, Touring).
  5. Exhaust: fit the M3 manifolds, catalysts and O2 sensors (label the sensor plugs so they can't be swapped), the secondary-air parts and the mid-pipes, then build the rear section around the new floor on the hangers from Phase 5 (Shop briefs). Keep every emissions part.
  6. Heat shields: fit shields over the muffler where it runs under the new floor, and near the tank, cargo floor and battery: the donor's, or ones you make.
  7. Clearance: with the suspension compressed, check that the exhaust clears the floor, tank and tires. One Touring's aftermarket Remus system (engine not stated) touched the inside of its 255/35-18 rear tires until the owner switched to 245/40-18 (e46fanatics).
  8. Fill and pressure-test the cooling system (Fluids and capacities). The S54's system pressure is 2 bar (Flow compilation, p.60); test it with a cooling-system pressure tester before the first start.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Cooling Holds 2 bar (29 psi) on the tester with no drop and no leaks Find and fix the leak before the first start
Exhaust Clears floor, tank and tires with the suspension compressed; heat shields fitted; new gaskets and nuts Re-hang, or modify the rear section
A/C New drier and O-rings; system closed and capped for the shop Replace any O-ring you reused
Sensor leads Every plug reaches its sensor without strain Extend or re-route it now

Phase 11: Wiring, immobilizer and coding

Work in this order. Pin lists, terminals and each coding session are in the Wiring and coding manual; the reasoning is in Electronics and coding. Steps 9 to 12 follow the manual's coding sessions 2 to 5, in order, and Phase 12 continues with sessions 6 to 9.

  1. Diagrams: BMW's WDS drawings for your build dates, on the public mirror the manual links, or ISTA by your VIN; the manual's tables name the drawing behind every wire (manual: engine harness and DME). Switch the ignition off whenever you plug or unplug a DME connector (BMW ST055).
  2. Engine harness and E-box: route the M3 engine harness, and fit its whole E-box branch with the fuse carrier, its gearbox-module harness and the DME. Compare each E-box plug (X6011, X6021, X6053 or X60531, X6054 or X60541, and X64101/X64102) with its mate, cavity by cavity, before you join them (E-box plugs): a mismatch at X6053 gave circuit-low codes on all six injectors in one swap (e46fanatics).
  3. Re-pin the body side from the move table for your DME: MS43 (to 02/2003) or MS45.1 (from 03/2003) (Harness and re-pinning). The moves cover the leak-test heater, the clutch signal, the cluster's three lamps and its start feedback, the brake-light switch's test contact and a recommended ground at X60004 pin 5, and on an MS45.1 the leftover wires in pins 5 and 15 (and 19, if fitted) come out. On an MS43, move the heater out of X6011 pin 7 before the clutch wire goes in. Re-pin; don't cut and splice. The four-wire move in older forum posts came from a different swap; on an MS43 Touring those wires move the other way (e46fanatics).
  4. Fuel-pump trigger: leave your Touring's pump wiring as it is. BMW's drawings put the K96 relay on X60004 pin 10 on both the MS43 and the MS45.1, and builders report the US MSS54 drives it from that pin; the M3's pump-module wire on X6021 pin 1 finds no mate in a 325i (Fuel-pump control, manual). Never connect the US DME's pulsed pump output to the relay. Expect a stored fault, location 1 (0x01), that shows in INPA but doesn't light the check-engine lamp (e46fanatics).
  5. Clutch, cruise and Sport button: fit the M3 clutch-switch module, 61319122700. The 325i's doesn't work with the MSS54's clutch chain, and two S54 builds cured no cruise with the M3 part (e46fanatics, NAM3). Its signal now crosses into the M3 harness at X6011 pin 7 and runs through the 420G's gear switch to X60002 pin 20; its wire to EWS pin 8, the start interlock fitted to US and Japanese cars, stays. The Sport button is optional (manual).
  6. Other body-side items: the rear-door LEDs in the M3 cluster; the heater valve, either your plain valve or the M3's with its auxiliary pump wired and coded, since an unwired pump made one builder's cooling hard to bleed (UNRIVALED – watch this before you S54 swap (2021), 02:32, manual); and the sensor leads from Phase 10.
  7. Check before first power-up: steps 1–19 of the manual's checks. With the battery off and the DME unplugged, prove every moved wire end to end and open to ground, and measure the coils, injectors, sensors and leak-test module. With the battery on, check the DME's feeds and grounds. Then, battery off again, the DME and every other module plugged in, measure the CAN bus: about 60 Ω between X60004 pins 36 and 37, back-probed at the DME, from BMW's two 120 Ω resistors (BMW ST040). Don't measure at the diagnosis socket: an E46's has no CAN pins, only the K-lines on pins 7 and 8 (WDS).
  8. First power-up: battery on a charger, ignition on, no cranking: steps 20–29. INPA should reach every module, the pump should prime once (STEUERN_EKP clicks K96), and the DME's status bits should follow the brake, the Sport button and, with a gear engaged, the clutch. The clutch module, brake switch and oil-level sensor are electronic, so these bits, not a meter, prove them.
  9. DME VIN (manual, Session 2): read the DME's VIN slots with Tool32 and check that the vendor wrote your Touring's full 17-character VIN. If not, write it into the next free AIF slot (ECUWorx). Inspection testers read the VIN from the DME (BMW SI B12 09 13).
  10. Immobilizer (Session 3): with an EWS-deleted DME there's nothing to sync; check that your EWS still reads the key and the clutch interlock (NAM3). The sync route works only with a virgin DME (Immobilizer (EWS)).
  11. Cluster (Session 4): fit the M3 cluster prepared to take your car's true mileage from the LCM (Instrument cluster and mileage). Never write a mileage into the LCM: it holds your car's record, and the replacement cluster must show the true mileage (49 U.S.C. §32704). On a pre-09/2001 (ZCS) car the LCM may not pass the mileage across, so check (e46fanatics).
  12. DSC (Session 5): code the unit you chose and initialise its VIN and steering angle (DSC, brakes and steering angle). This calibration is provisional; the final one comes after the alignment in Phase 12. If an M3 unit won't clear "VIN not initialized", refit your Touring's unit (option A), as one S54 Touring owner had to (e46fanatics).
  13. Bleed the DSC unit with INPA's routine if it was dry or replaced, using a firm pedal, then bleed conventionally again (Brakes and bleeding).
  14. Clear the faults in every module, cycle the ignition and read them again. Vehicle-order changes, body modules and headlight coding (Sessions 8 and 9) wait until the car runs, in Phase 12.

Watch: 50sKid – coding an E46 DSC unit (2016), 31:14, then the INPA VIN and steering-angle initialisation at 33:47; Tenza Motorsports – 5 things part 2 (2021), 08:45 wires the Sport button into a Touring.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Moved circuits Each reads continuity end to end, with no short to ground Re-seat the terminal; re-check the drawing for your car
CAN bus About 60 Ω between X60004 pins 36 and 37, with every module plugged in About 120 Ω: a terminating module unplugged or cut off (manual, step 19)
Modules INPA reaches the DME, cluster, LCM, EWS, DSC and the rest One module missing: its power, ground and bus wires. Several: the cable's pins 7 and 8
Warning lamps With the engine off, the red oil-pressure lamp and the charge lamp are lit Oil lamp out: the X60004 pin 11 wire to cluster pin 13, and the cluster's CAN_OELDRUCKSCHALTER coding (NAM3, manual)
Cluster Shows your car's true mileage (compare with your Phase 0 photo), with no tamper dot VIN mismatch between cluster and LCM (Troubleshooting)
DME VIN A generic OBD-II scanner shows your Touring's full VIN Write the AIF again
Clutch chain In Tool32, with a gear engaged, STAT_KRAFTSCHLUSS_EIN reads 1 with the pedal up and STAT_KUP_EIN 1 with it down; in neutral both stay fixed (MSS54 job reference) The M3 clutch module, the gear switch, the X6011 pin 7 crossing
Fuel pump One prime at key-on; STEUERN_EKP clicks K96 X60004 pin 10 to K96's coil wire (Fuel-pump control)
Fault memory Only location 1 (0x01), the pump fault, and, after a delete, possibly 130 (0x82) Fix every other fault before the first start (Troubleshooting)

Phase 12: Front end, first start and shakedown

Fit the front end in primer.

  1. Fit the grafted fenders, hood, headlights, kidney grilles and bumper with its carrier and supports; Tourings built from 09/2001 need the M3 bumper's fender supports added (Front end on a 4-door body). Keep the bumper's lower opening and ducting: the oil cooler breathes through it (MotoIQ).
  2. If the M3 headlights differ from your Touring's (halogen or xenon, levelling sensors), check their wiring now. Their light-module coding is the last coding session, at the end of this phase, and you aim them then, at ride height; don't drive after dark before that.

Before the first start.

  1. Fill the engine with 6.5 L (6.9 US qt) of 10W-60, BMW's figure after a rod-bearing job (BMW SI B11 04 04), and the power-steering system with the fluid marked on the M3 reservoir's cap (Fluids and capacities). Look under the car for drips.
  2. Charge the battery and keep it on a charger of at least 10 A for the whole session. One swap's first start failed on a flat battery (Tenza Motorsports – first start (2021), 03:55).
  3. Fit a mechanical oil-pressure gauge at the oil-pressure switch's port, which is threaded M12 × 1.5 (Flow compilation, p.58). On a tee, the switch stays connected and the red cluster lamp gives you a second reading.
  4. Fit the fuel-pressure gauge at the fuel-pressure fitting that TIS 13 31 shows (BMW ST041, part 2).
  5. Ignition on, engine off: in Tool32 load MSS54DS0.PRG, run IDENT and FS_LESEN, and save both results. This is step 1 of the manual's Session 6, which the rest of this phase follows (MSS54 job reference).

Prime the oil.

  1. Stop fuel and sparks: pull the fuel-pump relay (K96) and the injector relay (K6327, ETK), and unplug the six ignition coils. On the S54 the injector relay is what powers the injectors (BMW ST055).
  2. Test the start interlock: with the clutch up, the starter must not turn; with it down, it cranks. That's the clutch switch's wire to EWS pin 8, fitted to US and Japanese cars (BMW ST051).
  3. Crank in short bursts, with pauses to let the starter cool, until the gauge shows oil pressure and holds it while cranking and the red oil lamp goes out. The tachometer should move while you crank: the DME sees engine speed (the manual's steps 30–32).
  4. Refit the relays and the coil plugs.

Reset the adaptations and let the throttles learn. Do steps 2 to 5 of Session 6: clear the adaptations and the faults from cranking with the relays out, let the throttles run their key-on self-test, and check that STAT_WDK_ADAPT_WERT reads 0 and the clutch, brake and Sport inputs respond (BMW ST041, part 1). Then, with the key on, the pump primes: check 5.0 ± 0.2 bar (72.5 ± 2.9 psi), and look along the lines, the filter-regulator and the rail for leaks.

Start it.

  1. Start without touching the accelerator. Watch the oil gauge first: pressure must show at once. If it doesn't, switch off.
  2. Let it idle. Look for fuel, oil, coolant and power-steering leaks, and listen for knocking or VANOS rattle.
  3. Bleed the cooling system as it warms (Cooling and oil cooler), and check that the electric fan runs.
  4. At hot idle, check:
    • oil pressure at least 0.7 bar (10 psi), a figure from the Flow compilation, p.49 to confirm in TIS;
    • fuel pressure below 5 bar with the vacuum hose connected; BMW's diagram shows 3.8 bar with part-throttle vacuum (BMW ST041, part 2);
    • a steady idle, and fuel trims (STATUS_LAMBDA_ADD_1 and _2) near zero. A positive value means the DME is adding fuel to make up for a lean mixture (BMW ST041, part 3).
  5. Still warm, run the VANOS timing and leak jobs (Session 6, step 7) and compare them with the donor's readings from Phase 1; BMW's limits weren't found.
  6. Read the faults in every module. Expect only location 1 (0x01), the pump fault, and, after a delete, possibly the immobilizer's 130 (0x82), which doesn't stop the engine starting (NAM3). Fix anything else before you drive. If it starts and then dies, that isn't the immobilizer: see the manual's Won't start.
  7. Switch off, let it cool, top up the coolant and check the oil.
  8. Refit the oil-pressure switch. Take off the mechanical gauge and its tee and screw the switch back into its port at 27 Nm (20 ft-lb) (Torque values). At the next key-on the red lamp lights, and it goes out once the engine runs, with no oil at the switch.

Shake it down, align it, then finish the coding.

  1. Brakes: with a firm pedal, make progressive stops on a quiet road, then a careful hard stop from moderate speed. ABS should pulse and the car stop straight. Bleed again after the first few drives (NAM3). Bed in the parking-brake shoes on an empty road (Brakes and bleeding).
  2. Wheel speeds: in INPA's DSC status, all four should agree while rolling. An M3 built before 09/2002 has sensors that don't suit your harness, and one S54 Touring got an ABS light, never diagnosed, with non-M sensors in its M3 hubs (Tenza Motorsports – first start (2021), 07:18), so check this before you go further (DSC, brakes and steering angle).
  3. Short drives near home, then check the paint marks, wheel-bolt torque, fluid levels and fault memory.
  4. Fuel gauge: starting near empty, add a measured amount and check that the gauge, the range and Tool32's STATUS_TANKINHALT_LESEN follow it. Nobody has reported an M3 cluster on the Touring's tank senders, and the leak test relies on the reading (manual).
  5. Speedometer: compare with GPS. The M3 cluster over-reads by about 8.9% by design (e46fanatics).
  6. Alignment: torque the front rubber-bushed joints with the car's weight on its wheels, then have the car aligned to BMW's E46 M3 figures at its own loaded ride height (Wheel alignment, Shop briefs).
  7. Then calibrate the steering-angle sensor (Session 7) and run the DSC compensation routines, with the wheel straight, no pressure on the brake and the car level (DSC, brakes and steering angle). It comes after the alignment, not before, because the calibration takes the wheel's position at that moment as straight ahead (Evan Goyuk – E46 steering-angle calibration in INPA (2020), 02:48).
  8. Vehicle-order changes and body modules (Session 8): none need swap coding if you kept your Touring's vehicle order. If you change it, for example for M Track with an M3 MK60, code the other modules one at a time and reset the DME adaptations again.
  9. Headlights (Session 9): if the M3 headlights differ from your Touring's, code the light module for them. Then aim them, at ride height.
  10. A/C: have a shop vacuum-test the system and charge it by weight from the underhood label, then check it blows cold.

Break in the engine and set the readiness monitors.

  1. Drive 1,200 miles (1,930 km) below 5,500 rpm and 105 mph (169 km/h), BMW's figures after new rod bearings (BMW SI B11 04 04). Include closed-throttle coast-downs in gear, so the fuel cuts off: that's when the DME learns the crankshaft wheel, and its misfire detection is less sensitive until it has (BMW ST041, part 3).
  2. Checks during the break-in. BMW's bulletin gives none, and it says no oil service is needed at the end of the break-in (same SI). Our suggestion, logged on the break-in worksheet: at every fuel stop, the oil level and a look for leaks; after the first few drives, the paint marks, wheel-bolt torque, fluid levels and fault memory, and a second brake bleed; after the first few heat cycles, the coolant level; at about the halfway mark, every module's faults again; at 1,200 miles, the oil and filter.
  3. Change the oil and filter at the end of the break-in (our recommendation, not BMW's), and look for metal in the drained oil and the filter.
  4. Don't clear codes or disconnect the battery in the weeks before an inspection: both reset the readiness monitors (NYVIP3). Drive the MSS54 readiness plan in Inspection readiness and the manual. The fuel-tank leak test is the slow one: it runs with the engine off after at least 20 minutes of driving that followed at least 5 hours parked, with the tank 15–85% full, and only every second time those conditions are met (BMW ST041, part 3). The DME reads the fuel level from the cluster, so check the gauge first.

Watch: M346 Projects – Pt 13 first start (2024), 03:27 primes the oil before refitting the pump fuse; Tenza Motorsports – first start (2021), 05:38; Evan Goyuk – E46 steering-angle calibration in INPA (2020), 02:48.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Oil before firing The gauge shows pressure while cranking with fuel and sparks disabled Stop. Check the oil level, the pickup tube's O-ring and the pump before cranking again
Start interlock The starter turns only with the clutch down The clutch-switch wire to EWS pin 8
Throttles STAT_WDK_ADAPT_WERT = 0 after the reset; steady idle Repeat the reset with the battery on charge; check the throttle wiring
Shifts Revs drop cleanly between gears Rev hang: check the clutch-switch chain first, then that the DME runs manual software. In one non-M automatic-to-manual swap the cause was a tune written for an automatic (e46fanatics)
Hot oil pressure At least 0.7 bar at hot idle (compilation figure; confirm in TIS) Switch off and find the cause before you drive
Oil-pressure switch Refitted at 27 Nm with the gauge off; no oil at the port; the red lamp lights at key-on and goes out running Re-tighten; check the switch and its port for damage

Cooling Temperature steady, no air in the system, fan cycles Bleed again; with the M3 heater valve, check its pump's wiring and coding
Brakes and ABS Firm pedal; ABS pulses in a hard stop; the car stops straight Bleed again with INPA's routine; check the wheel-speed readings
Alignment Printouts before and after, with every value inside BMW's E46 M3 tolerances, set at the car's loaded ride height (Wheel alignment) A value the shop can't reach: a bent part, or a bushed joint torqued at the wrong height
Steering angle Calibrated after the alignment; INPA reads 0° with the wheel straight (Evan Goyuk – E46 steering-angle calibration in INPA (2020), 03:19); DSC lamp out Recalibrate; a sensor that won't hold 0° may be faulty (DSC, brakes and steering angle)
Break-in 1,200 miles below 5,500 rpm and 105 mph done; the checks along the way logged; oil changed; no metal in the oil or filter Metal: stop driving and find its source
Readiness A generic scanner shows every monitor ready. New York, for example, fails a 2001-or-later car with more than one not ready (NYVIP3) Fuel-tank leak test stuck: check its conditions, the leak-test wiring and the filler-neck seal (Inspection readiness)
Fault memory Only location 1 (0x01), the pump fault, and possibly 130 (0x82) after an EWS delete Troubleshooting

Phase 13: Paint, trim and paperwork

  1. Final bodywork: filler, guide coat, block sanding and panel gaps.
  2. Paint. Most builders paint the whole car so the new arches and front panels match. Brief the paint shop on what you've prepared, the colour, and whether the jambs and engine bay are included (Shop briefs).
  3. Fit trim and badges, the side skirts (with M3 rocker clips), the mirrors (on adapter bases if they're M3 mirrors) and the rear bumper with its quad-tip cut-outs (Rear bumper, skirts and trim).
    • The Touring's tailgate opening drops lower than the M3 bumper allows, so builders graft the Touring bumper's center section into the M3 bumper (Tracks and Trails – bodywork (2026), 28:00).
    • Facelift Touring doors use ribbon-cable mirror wiring; M3 mirrors need the older door harness (M346 Projects – Pt 21 (2024), 00:20).
    • If you flared the doors, their rubbing strips need reshaping to follow the flare (Calvin's Car Diary – PT9 (2020), 04:20).
  4. Pre-inspection check:
    • all lights, side markers and rear reflectors (keep the reflectors on the modified bumper), and headlight aim;
    • horn, wipers and washers;
    • brakes, parking brake and tires;
    • ABS, DSC, airbag and check-engine lamps light at key-on and then go out;
    • no exhaust leaks;
    • readiness monitors set, and a generic scanner reads your Touring's VIN from the DME.
  5. Inspection, registration and insurance (Registration and legal). In California the car goes to a BAR Referee first (BAR).
  6. Once it has passed, sell the fallback parts.

Watch: M346 Projects – making an E46 M3 Touring rear bumper (2024), 00:20.

Done when

Check Pass If it fails
Pre-inspection check Every item above passes Fix it before the appointment
Inspection Passed; in California, the Referee label fitted Fix what the inspector lists; for an emissions fail, see Inspection readiness
Paperwork Registration and title updated as your state requires; agreed-value insurance in place Keep receipts, photos and the donor's paperwork together for the insurer

Deep dive: chassis and drivetrain

This chapter explains how and why for each mechanical job. Torques appear at the step that needs them; the full table is in Torque values, oils and capacities are in Fluids and capacities, and single-use fasteners are in Consumables and single-use parts. Values marked "Flow" come from a forum compilation of RealOEM and BMW TIS printouts, not from BMW itself (Flow PDF, via NAM3). Where TIS gives a figure, use TIS. Group numbers such as "TIS 11 24" point you to BMW's own procedure.

Rear subframe mount reinforcement

What cracks. The floor panel the rear subframe bolts to (the rear axle carrier panel, RACP), not the subframe itself. Torque reversals from the diff flex the mounts; the spot welds fail first, then cracks spread through the floor (CMP, part 1). BimmerWorld describes the floor as two sheets with a gap between them, with the mount gussets tack-welded to the bottom sheet only, and says most cracks begin at three rosette welds on top of the rear-most mounts (BimmerWorld). Worn subframe bushings add movement and fatigue.

Why do it on a Touring. CMP says every E46 built after about February 2000 shares the same RACP, with cracks common on coupes, sedans, Tourings and convertibles (CMP, part 1, part 2). BMW's catalog agrees: the US 325i Touring, the sedan and the M3 coupe list the same RACP repair panel (41117000246), and the Touring and sedan share the trunk floor (41127062570), rails and battery tray. Only the rear-seat backrest differs, where a Touring cross member (41148253150) takes the place of the sedan's partition (ETK: floor, trunk floor, partition). So a 2007 forum claim that the wagon floor is "slightly different… for more cargo space" doesn't hold at the mounts, and its "the subframe mounting points are not reinforced" only means no stronger than the sedan's. Tourings do crack, among them an Alpina B3 3.3 Touring "with extensive subframe cracks" (e46fanatics). Your conversion feeds M3 torque into a body without the M3's extra gussets, weld points and C-pillar reinforcement (BMW ST041), and CMP says failures are worst on M3s. No source calls reinforcement mandatory, but by Phase 5 the exhaust, driveshaft, subframe and tank are already out, which is most of the labor.

Check your own floor. Look for cracks close to each mount, at the ends of the panel's V-shaped beam, along the spot welds joining the chassis rails to the boot floor, and along the RACP's front edge (CMP, part 2). A 2001–05 Touring has the later mount insert, which CMP identifies by two rectangular indents on the vertical face in front of the rear mounts; on these cars cracks spread through the RACP rather than tearing the insert out (CMP, part 1). If the floor is badly torn, BMW's repair is the whole 21.3 kg RACP (41117000246), a body-shop job, and US vendors list it as discontinued (ETK; Turner, unverified).

Kits and the Touring. No kit maker publishes Touring-specific cargo-side plates or instructions. The steel is the sedan's, so sedan-pattern plates should land in the same places, but dry-fit and trace before you cut. On one Touring, parts of a kit wouldn't fit because the Touring has no partition wall behind the rear seat (Calvin's Car Diary – PT15 (2025), 30:00).

Kit Touring fitment Cargo-side (topside) parts
CMP Stage 1 underside plates "All E46 non-M, E46 M3 (Excluding Hatch)" (CMP) None
CMP Stage 1.5 Chassis Rail Plates Same wording (CMP) Tie the RACP to the rails under the boot trim; 2.5 mm zinc-coated steel
CMP Stage 2 rear weld-in RACP beam Same wording; fit Stage 1.5 first (CMP) A beam welded in from above
CMP front weld-in beam No: coupes and sedans with split folding seats only (CMP) —
Redish/PS Designs V3 Not named; used on two Touring conversions (Autoalex – build part 3 (2023), 14:20, Tracks and Trails – Touring RACP (2025), 01:20) Four pieces fitted from above (Hack Engineering) and plates tying the RACP to the chassis legs; £180 for the plates plus £125 for six replica tank and brake-pipe brackets, July 2023 (Autoalex, 16:20)
Turner TDR4675412 Rogue lists the wagon (Rogue) New sheet metal for the mount tops (Turner, unverified)
Properparts 99–06 "Convertible, Coupe, Sedan and Wagon"; 16-gauge (1.5 mm) (Properparts) Not stated
DriftShop Competition Every E46 except the Compact; a buyer says there are no instructions (DriftShop) Not stated

Rogue RACP brace, Turner top-side brace Coupe and sedan only (Rogue via BimmerWorld; Turner, unverified) Bolt-in trunk braces

Sources disagree on whether the cargo-side work is needed on cars built after early 2000. PS Designs makes its trunk-floor plates optional on them (PS Designs/Redish); BimmerWorld says "Partial reinforcement (skipping the trunk portion) is not advised" (BimmerWorld). With M3 torque going in and the floor already open, do it.

Where the mounts sit. On the Touring, as on the sedan, the rear mounts sit under the front edge of the steel spare-wheel well, within about 120 mm (5 in) of each chassis rail, and the front mounts sit under the rear-seat base. In front of the well the RACP is spot-welded to the rails along a flange, and behind the mounts it joins the crumple structure on each side of the well (CMP, part 2). In the cargo area only trim covers this: Touring base panels, cover lids, a right-hand tray and side trims (ETK: cargo trim). If you take the well out (options 1–3 in Exhaust and the spare-wheel well), you reach the tops of the rear mounts through that opening instead of cutting separate windows.

Procedure (assembled from the Garagistic, Morehead, BimmerWorld and CMP instructions and two Touring builds). It is one sequence with the floor work in Exhaust and the spare-wheel well: the floor closes only after the axle and muffler have been tried in place.

  1. Prepare as in Welding settings and safety: fuel system and battery out, fire watch, fume extraction, test coupons. Strip the cargo trim, carpet, sound deadening and rear seat to bare steel.
  2. Measure the body diagonals before the first weld (same section).
  3. Strip the underside: exhaust, driveshaft, rear subframe and suspension, fuel tank, underbody panels and liners near the welds (Morehead). The plates can't be worked past the tank (Tracks and Trails – Touring RACP (2025), 02:00).
  4. Inspect all four mounts from below, as above.
  5. Clean to bare metal around each mount: paint, seam sealer and factory coating all come off. Clean mill scale off the plates and grind zinc coating off where you'll weld.
  6. Repair before reinforcing. Drill a small hole at the tip of any crack to stop it, weld the crack, and repair failed spot welds (Morehead).
  7. Underside plates. Each large plate fits one mount; test-fit and mark them. Tack the kit's spacers first, then the plates, with weld-through primer on their backs (Tracks and Trails – Touring RACP (2025), 07:20). Plug-weld through the plate's holes; over a double-skinned area, "drill through the first layer of sheet metal only" so the plate fuses to the structure behind (Garagistic K0536). Weld the perimeter in short stitches, moving between mounts to spread the heat.
  8. Open the floor by the cut rules in Exhaust and the spare-wheel well: never through the rail-to-RACP flange, or within about 120 mm of the rails, unless you'll rebuild it. If you keep the well, cut BimmerWorld's windows instead: butt the trunk plate against the seam across the floor, trace it, and cut about ¼ in (6 mm) inside the line (BimmerWorld).
  9. Topside welds. On each rear mount, weld the three rosette welds on top, then weld the upper and lower layers together at each existing spot weld (BimmerWorld). Stitch failed spot welds along the rail-to-RACP flange (CMP, part 2), then fit the rail plates: CMP's, or Redish's, which Tracks and Trails welded from inside the trunk (Tracks and Trails – Touring RACP (2025), 11:20). CMP's Stage 2 beam follows the rail plates.
  10. Front mounts. Their top welds sit under the rear-seat cushion, and CMP calls them "often neglected" (CMP, part 2). Tracks and Trails drilled out the rear-seat tabs to reach them and welded over the factory welds (Tracks and Trails – Touring RACP (2025), 05:40), and Redish reinforces "underneath the rear seat panel" on Tourings (Autoalex – build part 3 (2023), 04:00). Patch any opening you cut.
  11. V-brace mounting boxes, if you'll fit the M3 V-brace (below).
  12. Mock up before you close the floor. Hang the M3 rear axle and muffler in place, or leave the floor open until the Phase 6 mock-up, so the floor height and hangers suit the exhaust.
  13. Close the floor (see Exhaust and the spare-wheel well).
  14. Measure the diagonals again and compare them with your first readings.
  15. Protect it: epoxy primer, seam sealer on every joint (both sides), underbody coating outside and cavity wax inside (Tracks and Trails – Touring rear end (2025), 09:40). If you want BMW's structural foam in the mount cavities, inject it last: foam makes later welding "more difficult and dangerous" (BimmerWorld). Trim the cargo carpet if it no longer lies flat.

Weld, don't glue. Morehead: "Do not attempt to epoxy reinforcements… It does not work." BimmerWorld calls glue pointless because the mount tops must be welded anyway. One Touring builder bonded his plates with panel adhesive (M346 Projects – pt 14 (2024), 23:00); nobody has compared the two, and the kit instructions say weld.

Push rod, V-brace and spacers. The M3 subframe comes with a tubular steel push rod (front crossmember, 33312229295) in place of the non-M cast-aluminium part, bolted to the body with four M8 bolts (ETK: M3 carrier, Morehead). Fit it with your kit's spacer plates, which make up for the plate thickness; PS Designs calls them "ESSENTIAL" and gives 25 Nm for the four bolts (PS Designs/Redish). The M3 also has a V-shaped brace (33312229899) that "connects the rear axle carrier to the body" (BMW ST041), held by three M10×35 10.9 bolts at 59 Nm + 90° (ETK, PS Designs/Redish). Your body has nowhere to bolt it: "on the non-M models, there's nothing there" (Tracks and Trails – Touring rear end (2025), 06:00), and Redish says the Touring has grommets where the M3 has M10 threaded receivers (Autoalex – build part 3 (2023), 05:20). Builders added the mounting boxes in four ways: cut the donor's boxes off and plug-weld them at the factory weld points, a full day's work (Tracks and Trails, 06:20); bought an aftermarket lower box, "like a hundred bucks" (07:40); had new boxes fabricated (Autoalex v2 – update (2024), 04:20); or used the brackets in M3Projekts' kit (M3projekts – trunk floor (2023), 10:00). The boxes carry axle loads, so weld them as carefully as the plates, with the floor still open.

Stiffer subframe mounts. BimmerWorld recommends urethane or solid subframe mounts "even on street cars", noting that BMW's bracing of the front mounts only moved the stress elsewhere (BimmerWorld). Stiffer mounts pass the subframe's load into the floor more directly, so fit them only with the full underside and topside reinforcement.

Warning

Garagistic and Morehead recommend professional installation, and BimmerWorld recommends an experienced, professional shop. You will be welding thick plate to thin sheet, partly overhead. Practise on coupons first (see Welding settings and safety). Tracks and Trails spent about CAD 2,300 doing it himself, including a new welder, against a CAD 2,500 shop quote (November 2025), and concluded "for this, take it to a shop" (Tracks and Trails – Touring RACP (2025), 13:00). If you use a shop, give it the brief in Shop briefs.

Watch: Tracks and Trails – Touring RACP (2025), 07:20, Tedward – subframe cracks (2020), 10:57 (E46 M3 trunk-floor step; chapters only checked), O'Leary's – reinforcement done right (2024), 08:07, Condor – plate install, coupe (2019), description (Condor's kit lists the coupe and sedan only, not the Touring). Read: StanceWorks DIY and the photo instructions from Garagistic and BimmerWorld.

Exhaust and the spare-wheel well

On a non-M E46 the M3's transverse rear muffler sits where the spare-wheel well is. BMW had to make room on the M3 too: the coupe's boot floor was modified for the larger silencer, whose mounts sit "on the outer ends of the under body" (BMW ST041), and an M Mobility tire kit replaced the spare wheel to free space for a "40 liter half-shell muffler with four outlets" (BMW S54 module). Petroyle cuts the Touring's well out and inserts a carbon-fiber panel (Autocar).

Choose the floor:

Option What it takes Notes
1. The donor's M3 trunk floor Drill it out along its factory spot welds and weld it in at the same points The best fit: BMW built it for the same rails and RACP, with the muffler brackets (41122494844, 41112493931). A coupe's floor adds a front reinforcement (51717894229); a convertible's has two floor supports instead (ETK: coupe, convertible). A UK 318i Touring conversion used it (PistonHeads)
2. A new BMW M3 trunk floor (41127895619) As option 1, with new steel About £430 plus VAT in the UK in July 2023, one large panel including the battery box; Redish used it on two Tourings (Autoalex – build part 3 (2023), 13:20, Redish – Touring build part 1 (2022), 04:40). We found no US listing
3. A flat panel Cut the well out and weld in your own panel No tested design exists; follow the rules below
4. Keep the well The M3's front exhaust section, with a muffler in the Touring's position: Stromung's bolt-on, Supersprint's rear sections or a welded-on non-M muffler. A US builder of several S54 Tourings says most are done this way (e46fanatics) No floor surgery, but no quad-outlet M3 muffler. Tenza kept its non-M muffler with M3 flanges welded on, since the M3 pipe is 2³⁄₁₆ in against 2 in (Tenza – wagon exhaust (2021), 00:40); Project BMW fitted a ZHP muffler (Project BMW – wagon pulls hard (2021), 04:00)

Cut rules. The rear subframe mounts sit at the front corners of the well. CMP removes only the front 120 mm (5 in) of the well beside each rail to reach their tops, and the RACP is spot-welded to the rails along a flange in front of the well (CMP, part 2). So:

  • Keep the seam across the front of the well and the flanges along both rails, or drill out the factory spot welds and lift the whole panel out, as M346 did (M346 Projects – pt 14 (2024), 00:40).
  • Don't flame-cut or grind through the rail-to-RACP flange, or within about 120 mm of the rails, unless you plan to rebuild it.
  • Do the topside reinforcement through the opening before you close it (steps 8–13 of the reinforcement procedure).

Grafting an M3 floor (options 1 and 2), as M346 did on its Touring (M346 Projects – pt 14 (2024), 00:40):

  1. Drill out the same factory spot welds on the donor floor and on the Touring; "they all match up". Use a spot-weld chisel from below.
  2. Cut on the inside of the seams, leaving about 5 mm of flange to overlap or trim. The well is welded from underneath, so it drops out downward once it's free (02:40).
  3. Grind off seam sealer and the factory zinc coating where you'll weld (11:00).
  4. Test-fit again and again with the floor propped and strapped in place (08:20).
  5. Plug-weld at the original spot-weld positions (24:40), then grind, zinc-prime, seam-seal and paint (51:00).

Tracks and Trails kept more of the Touring floor and cut out only what the M3 panel replaces (Tracks and Trails – Touring rear end (2025), 01:20). Calvin's team called the floor graft one of the hardest jobs on their car (Calvin's Car Diary – PT6 (2020), 10:40).

Flat panel (option 3). No source gives the floor's gauge, so measure it at a cut edge and use steel no thinner. Stiffen the panel with swaged or bead-rolled ribs and set it at the height the mocked-up muffler needs.
